Dynamical systems as enriched functors
Suddhasattwa Das, Tomoharu Suda

Abstract
This article presents a general description of dynamical systems using the language of enriched functors and enriched natural transformations. This framework is essential to establish the equivalence of three descriptions of dynamics – a semigroup action on the domain; a parameterized family of endomorphisms; and a transformation of time-space into the collection of endomorphisms. A collection of categorical axioms are presented that provides a complete categorical language to develop dynamical systems theory. None of the assumptions are rooted in specific contexts such as topology and measure spaces. The equivalence of the three descriptions is further used to construct other related notions,such as transfer operators, orbits and shift-spaces. All of these objects are defined by their structural role and universal properties, instead of their usual pointwise definitions.



Key words.
Semi-conjugacy, enriched functor, semigroup, functor, adjoints, equalizer, orbits

AMS subject classifications.
18D25, 18A40, 37M99, 18F60, 37M22, 18A32, 18A25, 37M10

1  Introduction

A dynamical system consists of a collection
\Omega
of states, and a law describing a repeated transition between states along with the progress of time. The collection
\Omega
may be endowed with a sigma-algebra, a linear structure, a differential structure or a topology. If the dynamics law also conforms to these structures, e.g., it is measurable, linear, differentiable or continuous respectively, then one can study the dynamics in these different contexts. These contexts, namely ergodic, linear, differential and continuous dynamical systems have their own theories and constructions. The classical approach to dynamical systems theory is to discover the local structures within the state space, emerging as a consequence of the dynamics law.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Parameterized}\\
\text{endomorphisms}\\
\left[\text{Endo}\left(\Context\right)\right]_{\text{para}}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Enriched}\\
\text{functors}\\
\mathcal{T}\to\Context\\
\mathbb{F}_{\text{enrich}}\left(\mathcal{T};\Context\right)\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Left-}\\
\text{actions}\\
\left[\text{Left-action}\left(\Context\right)\right]\end{array}}
flow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
parametric
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(1)

Figure 1
:
Three functorial descriptions of dynamics.
One of the main results of the paper, formally stated in Theorem
7
, has been depicted here as a three way equivalence of categories.

An alternative to the classic approach is a categorical approach
(
BehrischEtAl2017dyn
;
fritz2020Stoch
;
MossPerrone2022ergdc
;
DasSuda2024recon
;
Suda2022Poincare
;
SchultzEtAl2020dyn
;
VagnerSpivakLerman2015
;
LibkindEtAl2021operadic
, e.g.)
, which takes a global view of dynamical systems. The narrative then takes a structural or synthetic form, in which various properties such as invariance, ergodicity and conjugacy have simpler and more intuitive definitions in terms of their structural roles, instead of being a consequence of their usual pointwise definitions. The synthetic description of category theory often makes several statements and proofs shorter, almost tautological. The complexity of the proof is transmitted into the challenge of establishing borne by the categorical structures.

One of the standard categorical descriptions of dynamics is as a functor
\Phi
from a semigroup into a context category. This provides the most direct and concise representation as a categorical object. We discuss later in diagram (
48
) that this simple idea allows the collection of linear, differentiable, topological and measurable dynamical systems, and Markov processes to become categories of its own, with a nesting of these categories in the order presented. Thus the various arrangements of dynamical systems created by different contexts and different time-structure are all categories, and are inter-related functorially. The description of a dynamical system as a functor encodes its algebraic property of being the action of a semi-group. However, this description is not adequate to encode the many other descriptions of a dynamical system, each suited for a specific purpose. A dynamical law may be presented as a map
\Phi:\mathbb{T}\times\Omega\to\Omega
, to directly indicate the dependence of the future state on the present time and state. Here
\mathbb{T}
represents some structure of time. These two interpretations of a dynamical system have lead to mostly separate lines of development of categorical dynamics, such as the former approach in
(
MossPerrone2022ergdc
;
DasSuda2024recon
, e.g.)
, and the latter as open dynamical systems
(
VagnerSpivakLerman2015
;
FongEtAl2016open
, e.g.)
.

There is yet a third representation of the dynamics as a parameterized collection of self-maps
\Phi^{\sharp}:\mathbb{T}\to\Endo(\Omega)
, where
\Endo(\Omega)
is the set of self-maps on
\Omega
. In this case
\Phi^{\sharp}
does not encode the algebra, but the nature of the dependence of the transformation on time. The vast body of theory in dynamical systems depends on our ability to use these descriptions interchangeably. It is easy to follow the equivalence of these descriptions within a set-theoretic context. However, the interchangeability is highly non-trivial in a general context. One of our main goals is to investigate the axiomatic roots of this interchangeability. This has been missing in most categorical descriptions of dynamics so far.

Our approach is distinguished by the utilization of enriched functors and enriched natural transformations. We present a set of purely category theoretic assumptions that support all the usual descriptions of dynamics, and also makes them equivalent. Due to our commitment to a structural or categorical description, no specific properties such as continuity, differentiability or measurability are assumed. Instead all properties are inherited from the categorical structure of the context, to be denoted as
\Context
. One of our main results is

Corollary 1
.

Under the categorical assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
, the three descriptions of a dynamical system - enriched functor, parameterized family, and left action of a semi-group, are equivalent, as displayed in Figure
1
.

Corollary
1
is a consequence of the more rigorous statement of Theorem
7
. All our results are built upon four basic categorical assumptions. Thus we present a synthetic approach to dynamical systems. The building blocks are not points and sets, but transitions and their inter-relations. Our axiomatic approach is outlined in Figure
4
. Figure
4
also displays four classical interpretations of dynamical systems. An important consequence of our axiomatization is

Corollary 2
.

Under the categorical assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
on a context category
\Context
, one can associate to every object
\Omega\in\Context
the notions of a path-space, orbit-space and a
\Context
-object representing the endomorphisms of
\Omega
.

While these notions are familiar and intuitive in a set-theoretic approach, they are rediscovered solely from categorical assumptions in this article. Their construction are defined in Section
4
. There is another set of constructions that we derive from our basic theorems, which are essential to dynamical systems theory. They are

Corollary 3
.

Suppose that the contexts of set theory, topology, measurable spaces and smooth spaces are represented by the closed monoidal categories

Set

,

CGWHS
,

QuasiBorel
and

Diffeology
respectively. Given a dynamical system
\left(\Phi^{t},\Omega\right)
in any of the contexts, one can associate to it two new dynamics within the same context :

1.

the pointwise action
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}^{t}
of the dynamics on curves

2.

the sub-flow
\shift^{t}
induced on the path space of the domain.

Both of these associations are functorial.

The two new induced dynamics –
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}^{t}
and
\shift^{t}
are in the same context as
\Phi
in the sense that the spaces and transformations lie in the same context category
\Omega
. The categories

CGWHS
,

QuasiBorel
and

Diffeology
are discussed in Section
7
. They provide the proper collection of transformations that satisfy the axioms mentioned above. The dynamics of
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}^{t}
and
\shift^{t}
are well familiar in classical dynamical systems theory as the transfer operator and shift operators on the space of paths. These are usually defined by their pointwise actions. Corollary
3
presents them as a purely categorical consequence. The details are presented in Theorems
6
and
9
respectively. These dynamics could be defined in any setting which lack an apriori notion of points or states.

A major gain from the perspective of enriched categories is a refined notion of orbits. An orbit of a point is the succession of states that it transitions to. The phase space is a disjoint collection of orbits, and the complexities of the dynamics lie in their mutual arrangement. Orbits have even been suggested as a primitive object in the description of dynamics
(
Suda2023dynamical
;
Suda2023partial
, e.g.)
. Another main contribution of the perspective of enriched category is the notion of the space of paths
\PathF(\Omega)
. This notion in turn allows the notion of a
subshift
:

Corollary 4
.

One can associate to a dynamical system
\left(\Phi^{t},\Omega\right)
in any of the contexts in Corollary
3
, a subshift dynamics
\left(\shift_{\Phi}^{t},E_{\Phi}\right)
within the same context. The subshift is defined via the following universal construction –

1.

the domain is the maximal collection of states which generate the same orbits under
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}^{t}
and
\shift^{t}
.

2.

the dynamics under
\shift_{\Phi}^{t}
commutes with both
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}^{t}
and
\shift^{t}
.

Corollary
4
is stated in more technical terms in Theorem
11
. Each point in a subshift is the forward orbit of some point in
\Omega
. We further go on to prove that a subshift is precisely an equivalent description of the original dynamics, in terms of its orbits :

Corollary 5
.

The domain of the subshift is an isomorphic copy of the domain
\Omega
,embedded in the path space
\PathF(\Omega)
if the time semigroup is commutative.

Corollary
5
will be derived as an immediate consequence of Theorems
11
and
12
. These constructions are introduced in Section
5
, and their properties are stated rigorously later in Theorem
6
and Theorem
9
. As mentioned before, because our approach is synthetic / constructivist instead of analytical / descriptive, these results are proven without relying on details specific to topology, measure theory and stochastic processes.

Outline

We begin with a brief review of category theory in Section
2
. We introduce the first axiom that enables a categorical description of dynamical systems. Time is assumed to be a semigroup from the outset. Our next axioms in Section
3
imparts a finer structure to the time semigroup. They also enable self-maps and paths to be interpretable as new objects within the same category. Our fourth and final axiom is presented in Section
4
. It provides the connection between all the three descriptions of dynamical systems. These four axioms enable a deeper understanding of constructs such as path spaces and transfer operator. We analyze these in Section
5
. Next in Section
6
we incorporate the study of states and stationary states, which generalize the notion of both invariant sets and measures. We end with some applications and discussion in Section
7
.

2  Dynamics as a functor

Table 1
:
Various kinds of dynamical systems. Section
2
presents how the collection of dynamical with a given time-structure
\mathcal{T}
and mathematical context
\mathcal{C}
has the structure of a functor-category. The table presents a few instances of
\mathcal{T}
and
\Context
. Each leads to a different sub-branch in the theory of dynamical systems. A categorical approach unifies many observations in these separate branches.

\mathcal{G}

Interpretation

\mathcal{C}

Interpretation

Invertible flow, continuous time

Topo

Topological dynamics

\left[\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right]

Continuous time

Meas

Measurable dynamics

\mathbb{N}

Discrete time

\mathbb{Z}

Invertible, Discrete time

\mathbb{T}^{d}

Torus actions

Set

Set-theoretic dynamics

As mentioned before, every notion of a dynamical system has two ingredients - a collection of
states
, called the
domain
or
phase-space
, and a collection of transformations of the domain. The simplest example is when the domain
\Omega
is a set, i.e., an unstructured collection of states, and the dynamics is a collection of maps from
\Omega
to itself. This is a set-theoretic dynamical system, and is listed along with several other kinds of dynamics in Table
1
. Dynamical systems theory usually assumes some structure on
\Omega
, such as measurability, probability, linearity or topology, and expects that the transformations respect these properties. For the examples listed, this means that the transformations are measurable, measure-preserving, linear and continuous respectively. One also assumes a certain algebraic structure on the transformations, which allows the transformations to be applied repetitively. Each of the collection of structure-preserving self-transformations are called
endomorphisms
. Endomorphisms have a semigroup structure to them. The term “endomorphism(s)” always refers to such a semigroup created by some structural aspect of the domain
\Omega
. A dynamical system is tied to the semigroup property of endomorphisms.

First description

A dynamical system is the action
\tilde{\Phi}
of a semigroup
\mathcal{T}
on the domain
\Omega
. Thus
\tilde{\Phi}
is homomorphism from
\mathcal{T}
to the semigroup of endomorphisms of
\Endo(\Omega)
.
The time-semigroup
\mathcal{T}
could be the additive semigroups
\left[\real{}_{0},+\right]
,
\left[\real,+\right]
,
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
and
\left[\mathbb{N},+\right]
, and even rotation groups such as the torus group
\mathbb{T}^{d}

(
raissy2010torus
;
Das2023Koop_susp
, e.g.)
. One of the main considerations of dynamical systems theory is that these systems co-exist, often in a nested manner. Most of the complicated behavior in dynamical systems theory is an outcome of the interplay between various co-existing sub-systems
(
DSSY_Mes_QuasiP_2016
;
DasJim17_chaos
;
AlexanderEtAl1992
;
newhouse2004new
;
LiaoEtAl2018
, e.g.)
. The description of individual dynamical systems along with their inter-connections, become more concise when one uses the language of
category theory
.

Category

A category
\mathcal{C}
is a collection of two kinds of entities :

1.

objects : usually representing different instances of the same mathematical construct;

2.

morphism : connecting arrows between a pair of objects equipped with compositionality : given any three objects
a,b,c
of
\mathcal{C}
and two morphisms
a\xrightarrow{f}b
and
b\xrightarrow{g}c
, the morphisms can be joined end-to-end to create a composite morphism represented as
a\xrightarrow{g\circ f}b
. This composition is also associative;

3.

identity morphism : each object
a
is endowed with a morphism
\Id_{a}
called the
identity
morphism, which play the role of unit element in composition.

Given two objects
x,y
in
\mathcal{C}
, the collection of arrows from
x
to
y
is denoted as
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x;y)
. If the category
\mathcal{C}
is obvious, then it will be dropped from the notation and
\Hom(x;y)
will be used for simplicity. Note that this collection may be infinite, finite or even empty. The last criterion implies that for each
x

\Hom(x;x)
has at least one member. Whenever there are multiple categories being discussed, one uses the notations
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x;y)
or
\mathcal{C}(x;y)
to indicate that the morphisms are within the category
\mathcal{C}
.

Examples

One of the most fundamental categories is

Set

, the category in which objects are sets up to a certain prefixed cardinality, and arrows are arbitrary maps. Similarly

Topo

denotes the category of topological spaces, with continuous maps as arrows. We denote by

Vec

the category in which the objects are vector spaces and arrows are linear maps. The collection
\left[\text{Aff}\right]
that we have already defined has the same objects as

Vec

but all affine maps as morphisms. Note that this includes the morphisms in

Vec

. This makes

Vec

a
subcategory
of
\left[\text{Aff}\right]
. Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is any set. Then the power-set
2^{\mathcal{U}}
of subsets of
\mathcal{U}
is a category, in which the relations are the subset
\subseteq
relations. Note that there can be only at most one arrow between any two objects
A,B
of this category, which is to be interpreted as inclusion. Such categories are known as
preorders
, and other examples are the category of ordered natural numbers, real numbers, open covers, and the concept of infinitesimal
(
Das2023CatEntropy
, see)
.

A particularly important example of categorical structure can be found in semigroups. Any semigroup
\mathcal{G}
can be interpreted as a 1-point category, in which each arrow corresponds bijectively to elements of
\mathcal{G}
. The associativity of semigroup operation becomes the associativity of arrow composition. Figure
2
show the 1-point categorical representations of the semigroups
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
,
\left[\mathbb{Z},+\right]
and
\left[\real,+\right]
. We formalize this into an assumption :

Assumption 1
.

There is a semigroup
\mathcal{T}
, interpreted as a 1-object category.

Figure 2
:
Representing semigroups as 1-object categories –
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
,
\left[\mathbb{Z},+\right]
and
\left[\real,+\right]
.

Any dynamical system has a concept of time associated to it. The role of time can be played by any semigroup, such as
(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)
,
(\mathbb{N},+)
,
(\real_{0},+)
and
(\real,+)
. Such a time semigroup shall be denoted by
\mathcal{T}
. We shall interpret it as a 1-point category. Next let us fix a category
\Context
and call it the
context
category. It is usually taken to be

Topo

,

Vec

or

Meas

. The category
\Context
represents the nature of spaces and maps that are involved our dynamical systems of interest. The context and time are the two ingredients of a dynamical systems.

Functors

Given two categories
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
, a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a mapping between their objects along with the following properties :

1.

For each
x,y\in ob(\mathcal{C})
,
F
induces a map
F_{x,y}:\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x;y)\to\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(Fx;Fy)
. Thus arrows / morphisms between any pair of points get mapped into arrows between the corresponding pair of points in the image.

2.

F
preserves compositionality : given any three objects
a,b,c
of
\mathcal{C}
and two morphisms
a\xrightarrow{f}b
and
b\xrightarrow{g}c
,
F(g\circ f)=F(g)\circ F(f)
.

3.

F
preserves identity :
F(\Id_{a})=\Id_{F(a)}
.

Functors are thus maps that also preserve categorical structure, or in other words, preserves compositionality. One of the simplest notions of functors are monotonic functions from to , and may be interpreted as functors from
\left[\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right]
to
\left[\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right]
or
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
. Realizing mathematical transformations as functors leads to deeper insights in the associated field. Some examples are Lebesgue integration
Leinster_integration_2020
, diameter and Lebesgue number
Das2023CatEntropy
, and topological closure
ClementinoTholen1997sep
;
ClementinoGiuliTholen1996
. This prompts our first interpretation :

Definition 1

(Interpretation 1 - Semigroup action)
.

Under Assumption
1
, a dynamical system within a context category
\Context
is a functor
\tilde{\Phi}:\mathcal{T}\to\Context
.

Any functor from a 1-object category such as
\mathcal{T}
has a single object in its image. The choice of this object corresponds to the choice of the domain
\Omega
of the dynamics. Next involved is a family of endomorphisms
\Phi^{t}:\Omega\to\Omega
indexed by
\left\{t\in\mathcal{T}\right\}
. The compositionality of a functor is precisely an action of the semigroup
\mathcal{T}
on endomorphisms of
\Omega
. See Figure
3
for an illustration. As mentioned before, the relations between dynamical systems are equally important as the dynamics within individual systems. These relations can be expressed concisely in categorical language as a transformation of functors.

Natural transformations

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be two categories, and
F,F^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be two functors. A natural transformation
\alpha
from
F
to
F^{\prime}
, denoted as
\alpha:F\Rightarrow F^{\prime}
is a family of arrows
\left\{\alpha_{c}:F(c)\to F^{\prime}(c)\;:\;c\in ob(\mathcal{C})\right\}
such that the following commutations hold

(2)

Just like arrows / morphisms connect objects, natural transformations connect functors. In fact the collection
\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{C}\;;\;\mathcal{D}\right)
of functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
is itself a category, in which the morphisms are natural transformations. This realization that the collection of functors itself is a category bears a lot of significance in the study of homology, simplicial complexes and sheaf theory in general. We will find a special significance in the study of dynamical systems.

The collection of dynamical systems evolving according to time interpretation
\mathcal{T}
and within the context
\Context
, forms the functor category

\left[\text{DS}\right]:=\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right).

Each object in this category is a functor
\tilde{\Phi}:\mathcal{T}\to\Context
. This involves selecting an object
\Omega
from
\Context
. A morphism in
\mathbb{F}\left(\mathcal{T}\;;\;\Context\right)
is a natural transformation
h
. Due to the special structure of
\mathcal{T}
, the commutation in (
2
) becomes

The diagram shown here is called a
semi-conjugacy
in classical dynamical systems theory. It is a generalization of the notion of change of variables. If
h
is injective, it leads to an embedding of the dynamics of
\Phi^{t}
within
\Phi^{\prime t}
. If
h
is surjective, it means that the dynamics of
\Phi^{\prime t}
is
factored
within
\Phi^{\prime t}
.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

Figure 3
:
Dynamics as a functor. Section
2
presents a dynamical system as a functor between a semigroup category
\mathcal{T}
representing time, and a category
\mathcal{C}
representing the context. Shown here is such a functor
\tilde{\Phi}
transforming the additive semigroup
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
of non-negative integers into self-maps on a space
\Omega
. Since
\left[\mathbb{N}_{0},+\right]
is generated by the unit element
1
, the image
F
of this element generates all iterations of the dynamics.

This completes the very basic step of our categorical language - interpreting dynamics as functors and semi-conjugacies as natural transformations. We next present the need of the finer structure of enriched categories and functors.

3  Time as an enriched category

In the first description of dynamics, time is merely assumed to be a semigroup. There is no additional assumption imposed on the collection of time-instants, which could make them interpretable in context
\Context
. In most studies of dynamical systems, the dependence of the transformation of time is assumed to be more than just the result of the homomorphism
\tilde{\Phi}
. For example in ODE-theory, the flow
\Phi
is differentiable w.r.t. time, and in the study of operator semigroups, and in measurable dynamics, the dependence on time is measurable too. These properties cannot be guaranteed by Assumption
1
alone. To enable the time-semigroup itself to be present as an object of the context category
\Context
, we need to rely on advanced categorical notions such as monoidal categories and enriched categories.

A
monoidal category
is a category equipped with a product like structure
\otimes
, called
tensor product
. It allows objects multiple objects
a,b,c,\ldots
to be combined into a new object
a\otimes b\otimes c\otimes\ldots
. A tensor product is a bifunctor which generalizes the usual notion of categorical product. This operation is associative up to isomorphism, and has a identity element called the
unit object
, and is denoted by
1_{\Context}
. A monoidal category is said to be
symmetric
if the tensor product is symmetric up to a natural isomorphism. i.e., there exists a natural isomorphisms
\text{swap}_{a,b}:a\otimes b\to b\otimes a
. Our next assumption will be :

Assumption 2
.

The context category
\Context
is a symmetric monoidal category, and
\mathcal{T}
is enriched over
\Context
.

Many classical settings of dynamical systems theory, such as the category of topological spaces and continuous maps, are closed monoidal categories. Further, the theory of Markov processes can also be developed within a symmetric monoidal category.

Definition 2

(Category of Markov processes)
.

The category

Stoch

contains as objects measurable spaces
(\Omega,\Sigma)
, and Markov transitions as morphisms. Thus a morphism from
(\Omega,\Sigma)
to
(\Omega^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
is a map
k:\Omega\times\Sigma^{\prime}\to[0,1]
such that

1.

for each
\omega\in\Omega
,
k(\omega,\cdot):\Sigma^{\prime}\to[0,1]
is a probability measure;

2.

for each
S\in\Sigma^{\prime}
,
k(\cdot,S):\Omega\to[0,1]
is a measurable function.

The composite of two kernels
k,k^{\prime}
is the kernel
k^{\prime\prime}
depicted below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omega^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omega,\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Sigma^{\prime\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

defined as

k^{\prime\prime}(\omega,S)=\int_{\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\prime}}k^{\prime}(\omega^{\prime},S)dk(\omega,d\omega^{\prime}),\quad\forall\omega\in\Omega,\,S\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}.

Recall that a category
\mathcal{X}
is said to be
enriched
over the monoidal category
\Context
if the following are satisfied :

1.

for every
a,b\in ob(\Context)
, there is an object
\mathcal{X}(a,b)\in ob(\Context)
;

2.

for every
f\in\Hom_{\mathcal{X}}(a;b)
, there is a morphism
f_{\Context}\in\Hom_{\Context}\left(1_{\Context};\mathcal{X}(a,b)\right)
. If
a=b
and
f=\Id_{a}
, then the corresponding morphism is denoted as
\eta_{a}:1_{\Context}\to\mathcal{X}(a,a)
;

3.

for every
a,b,c\in ob(C)
, there is a map
\circ_{a,b,c}\in\Hom_{\Context}\left(\mathcal{X}(b,c)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,b);\mathcal{X}(c,a)\right)
such that the following three commutations hold :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{X}(c,d)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(b,c)\right)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(b,d)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(a,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(c,d)\otimes_{\Context}\left(\mathcal{X}(b,c)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,b)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(c,d)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,c)}
assoc
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{b,c,d}\otimes_{\Context}\Id_{\mathcal{X}(a,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{a,b,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id_{\mathcal{X}(c,d)}\otimes_{\Context}\circ_{a,b,c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{a,c,d}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(b,b)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(a,b)}
left identity
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id_{b}\otimes_{\Context}\Id_{\mathcal{X}(a,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{a,b,b}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(a,b)\otimes_{\Context}1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(a,b)\otimes_{\Context}\mathcal{X}(a,a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(a,b)}
right identity
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id_{\mathcal{X}(a,b)}\otimes_{\Context}\Id_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{a,a,b}}

In our case, the 1-object category
\mathcal{T}
plays the role of the general category
\mathcal{X}
. This implies the following :

1.

the hom-set

\mathbb{T}:=\Hom_{\mathcal{T}}(*,*)

(3)

is an object in the context category
\Context
.

2.

For every morphism
t\in\Hom_{\mathcal{T}}(*,*)
, there is a
\Context
-morphism
f_{t}\in\Hom_{\Context}\left(1_{\Context};\mathbb{T}\right)
. When
t=\Id_{*}
, we denote
f_{t}
by
\eta_{\star}
.

3.

The object
\mathbb{T}
is a monoid, i.e., there is a
\Context
morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\add}

such that the following commutations hold :

\hbox to185.5pt{\vbox to52.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 92.74995pt\lower-25.87357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(4)

The first diagram establishes the associativity of the composition rule. The second two diagrams uphold some basic rules of combinations of
\eta_{\star}
and
\add
. Together, these commuting diagrams preserve the algebraic structure of
\mathcal{T}
within
\mathcal{C}
. The last two commutation loops is reminiscent of the idea of a
dynamical system over a monoid action

BehrischEtAl2017dyn
. The ideas present in that categorification of dynamical systems relies on a complicated algebraic structure of time. Our construct of time is significantly simpler, and the algebraic properties of the action of time is encoded within the enriched structure of the functor
\tilde{\Phi}
.

One major consequence of Assumption
2
is the interpretation provided by (
3
). Our initial interpretation of time was an abstract semigroup. Each element of that semigroup, or equivalently, each endomorphism of the unique object of
\mathcal{T}
is interpreted as time instants. It is only Assumption
2
which enables the loose collection
\Endo\left(\star_{\mathcal{T}}\right)
of endomorphisms to be realized as an object in
\Context
itself.

Closed monoidal structure

We are accustomed to viewing functional spaces such as endomorphisms and path-spaces as topological spaces of their own. The collection of maps between two sets is itself a set. Our next assumption generalizes this property for the context
\Context
:

Assumption 3
.

The context category
\Context
is a closed symmetric monoidal category.

While Assumption
2
already assumed
\Context
to be monoidal, Assumption
3
makes the additional assumption of
\Context
being closed too. Recall that being
closed, monoidal
means that for every two objects
y,z\in\mathcal{C}
there is a special object
\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}
called the
exponential
object or
internal-hom
object. It is defined to be the right adjoint to the monoidal product. It has the following natural bijection between hom-sets

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(y\otimes x;z\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(x;\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}\right).

(5)

Taking
x=1_{\mathcal{C}}
in (
5
) implies

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(y;z\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(1_{\mathcal{C}};\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}\right).

(6)

Equation (
6
) explains how the object
\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}
is an alternate means of describing the Hom-set
\Hom(y;z)
. The elements of the object
\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}
are in bijective correspondence with the collection of morphisms from
y
to
z
. Now suppose that we take
x=\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}
in (
5
). Then we get a bijection

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(y\otimes\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}};z\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}};\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}\right),\quad\text{eval}_{y,z}\mapsto\Id_{\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}}.

(7)

As indicated, there is a morphism
\text{eval}_{y,z}:y\otimes\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}\to z
which corresponds to the identity morphism of
\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}
. This is called the
evaluation
morphism of
y,z
. Intuitively, the evaluation morphism
\text{eval}_{y,z}
assigns to every
internal function

\phi\in\left[y\,;\,z\right]_{\text{int}}
, and evaluation point in
y
, a point in
z
. Evaluation morphisms are a categorical realization of the action of usual set-maps, but in a general categorical context.

We present two useful examples of closed monoidal categories.

Definition 3

(Contractive maps 1)
.

The category

Metric

of metric spaces and contractive maps are closed with respect to their Cartesian products. This is because the collection of contractive maps between two metric spaces
X,Y
is itself a metric space with the supremum norm.

Definition 4

(Contractive maps 2)
.

The collection of compact metric spaces form a sub-category of

Metric

. It also preserved the closure property
(
Leinster2022eventual
;
BuragoEtAl2001metric
, see)
, and hence is another instance of a closed monoidal category.

Next we take note of two related notions of adjunction, which will be crucial in our discussion.

Definition 5

(Two-types of adjoints)
.

Let
\Phi:\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega\to\Omega
be a morphism in a closed symmetric monoidal category. Since
\Context
is symmetric and closed, we can take two kinds of adjoints of
\tilde{\Phi}
:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\sharp}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{T}\to\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}

(8)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\flat}

\displaystyle:\Omega\to\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}.

We call the former the
sharp adjoint
and the latter the
flat adjoint
.

The sharp adjoint corresponds to the usual identification of a flow
\Phi(t,x)
with a family of endomorphisms
\Phi^{t}
. It raises the time variable. Similarly, the flat adjoint corresponds to the identification with a family of curves
\Phi_{x}
starting from the initial point
x
.

Enriched objects

Assumption
3
enables many essential constructs of dynamical systems theory to be realized as objects of
\Context
. Pick an element
\Omega
from
\Context
. An important object is

\Endo(\Omega):=\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}},

(9)

the endomorphism object associated to domain
\Omega
. It allows the collection of self maps of
\Omega
to be interpreted as an object in
\Context
. Another important consequence is the creation of an object

\PathF(\Omega):=\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}},

(10)

which we interpret as the
path-space
of
\mathcal{T}
-indexed paths in domain
\Omega
. It is the collection of all possible
orbit
-s on the domain
\Omega
, not limited to those generated by the dynamics of
F
. Due to Assumption
2
, this collection is also of the same type / category as objects in
\Context
. It is alternatively known as the space of
sample-paths
and is a fundamental object of study in the theory of stochastic processes
(
Doob1953book
, e.g.)
.

The time object
\mathbb{T}
and endomorphism object
\Endo(\Omega)
together enable two new definitions of a dynamical system :

Definition 6

(Interpretation 2. Parametric endomorphisms)
.

A dynamical system is a parameterized family of maps
\Phi^{\sharp}:\mathbb{T}\to\Endo(\Omega)
.

Thus this interpretation of a dynamical system is as a morphism

\Phi^{\sharp}:\mathbb{T}\to\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}

(11)

Now note that by Assumption
2
,
\Phi^{\sharp}
has an adjoint morphism

\Phi:\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega\to\Omega.

(12)

Definition 7

(Interpretation 3. Pre-flow)
.

A dynamical system is a pre-flow on the domain
\Omega
, meaning a morphism / map
\Phi:\mathbb{T}\times\Omega\to\Omega
.

Thus we have two different interpretations of dynamics- as a parameterized family
\Phi^{\sharp}
of self-maps (
11
) – and as as a pre-flow (
12
).
We shall impose some algebraic conditions on a pre-flow to obtain a
flow
or a
left-action
of a semi-group, in Definition
9
.
At this point the three descriptions are different and not interchangeable, in a general category
\Context
. For example, the flow map
\Phi^{t}
generated by a vector field leads to an action of on the group of diffeomorphisms, and the dependence on
t
is smooth. However, a time rescaling such as
t\mapsto\Phi^{t^{2}}
is still a dynamical system according to Interpretations 2 and 3, but not according to Interpretation 1. We shall need more assumptions on
\Context
and
\tilde{\Phi}
to enable these descriptions to be interchangeable. We end this section by presenting three important dynamical systems that can be constructed out of a pre-flow
\Phi
.

Secondary dynamics

We assume Assumptions
2
and
3
hold in the following constructions.

1.

Shift map.
One of the most important instances of a flow is provided by a natural action of time on the elements of the path space. Recall from (
10
) that
\PathF(\Omega)
is just the internal hom object
\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}
. Consider the following composite morphism shown as a dashed arrow below on the left :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\add\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{eval}_{T,\Omega}}
right adj.
left adj.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift_{\Omega}}

(13)

By (
5
) this arrow corresponds naturally to the dashed arrow shown above on the right. Thus for each object
\Omega
, the path space
\PathF(\Omega)
has a natural dynamics in the sense of Description
7
:

\shift:\mathbb{T}\otimes\PathF(\Omega)\to\PathF(\Omega).

The shift map
\shift
acts on the element of the path-space via
\add
. The morphism
\add
contains the semigroup composition rule of
\mathcal{T}
. Equation (
13
) reveals how this is transformed into a compositional rule on the paths with
\mathcal{T}
-structure. Theorem
6
presents the functorial nature of this morphism.

2.

Transfer operator
Now take any object
X\in\mathcal{C}
. Equation (
12
) leads to the following composite morphism shown on the left :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[X\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes X\otimes\left[X\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{swap}\otimes\left[X\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\text{eval}_{X,\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
right adj.
left adj.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[X\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Tr}_{\Phi}}

(14)

The other two morphisms shown above are obtained by successive adjoint operations. We call
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}
the
transfer operator
corresponding to the dynamics
\tilde{\Phi}
. The object
\left[X\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}
can be interpreted as the collection of
X
-patterns in
\Omega
. The action of
\tilde{\Phi}
on
\Omega
induces an action on such patterns. Theorem
9
presents the functorial nature of this morphism.

3.

Koopman operator
Similar to the transfer operator, one has a Koopman operator, which act on patterns from
\Omega
into an object
X
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\otimes\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{swap}\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{eval}_{X,\Omega}}
right adj.
left adj.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\Omega\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\Phi}}

In other words
U_{\Phi}
is a pre-flow in the sense of Definition
7
.

So far we have seen how an additional structural assumption on
\Context
leads to an interpretation of the collection of endomorphisms and paths as objects of
\Context
itself. This also enables the creation of the
\shift
morphism, which is a dynamical system according to the interpretation in Definition
7
of a dynamical system. We have also seen the creation of the transfer and Koopman operators, which are dynamical systems not according to Definition
1
but according to Definitions
6
and
7
. To make the two new interpretations, along with
\shift
, Koopman and transfer morphisms conform with Definition
1
, we need yet another structural assumption, to be described next.

4  Enriched functors

Interpretation I.
Dynamics as a functor
\Phi:\mathcal{T}\to\Context

Assumption
1
: Time is a semigroup
\mathcal{T}
, equivalently, a 1-object category

Set theoretic dynamics
\Phi:\mathcal{T}\to\textbf{ \text{Set} }

Assumption
2
: context
\Context
is closed, monoidal

Assumption
3
: time
\mathcal{T}
enriched over
\Context

Assumption
4
: dynamics functor
\Phi
is enriched

Time as a
\Context
-object
\mathbb{T}
(
3
)

Self-maps
\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}
of domain
\Omega

Path space
\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}

Interpretation II.
Dynamics as a parameterized family
\hat{\Phi}:\mathbb{T}\to\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}
of self-maps (
11
).

Interpretation III.
Dynamics as a flow
\tilde{\Phi}:\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega\to\Omega
on domain
\Omega
(
12
).

Interpretation IV.
Dynamics as a shift map
\shift_{\Phi}
(
32
) embedded in the path-space
\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}
.

adjoint
composition with evaluation

Figure 4
:
Many interpretations of dynamical systems. Dynamics are interpreted in different ways in different areas of mathematics. We start by interpreting dynamics as the action of a semigroup. The diagram depicts how this interpretation could lead to three other interpretations
\Phi^{\sharp}
,
\Phi
and
\shift_{\Phi}
respectively. This is done by combining with different assumptions on the context category
\Context
, time semigroup
\mathcal{T}
, and functor
\tilde{\Phi}
. These assumptions shown in the center of the diagram are categorical in nature. They lead to the basic concepts shown in dark boxes. Any discussion of a dynamical system requires a consideration of three associated objects - the abstract algebraic notion of time; the collection of
\mathcal{T}
-indexed paths in
\Context
; and the collection of self-maps on a domain
\Omega
with
\Context
. If
\Context
is closed, all of these can now be interpreted as objects of
\Context
itself. The arrows in the diagram represents logical implications. Each box represents a concept or interpretation. It can be constructed by assuming the contents of its preceding boxes. Thus the interpretation
\Phi^{\sharp}
as a parameterized family requires the concept of an time-object, self-map object, and the assumption of
\tilde{\Phi}
being an enriched functor. The interpretation
\Phi
as a pre-flow then follows via an adjoint relation guaranteed by the closed monoidal structure of
\mathcal{C}
. The interpretation
\shift_{\Phi}
as a sub-shift is a direct consequence of the pre-flow-interpretation, time-object and self-maps. The top-left corner of the diagram shows that in fact, if the context
\Context
can be placed in

Set

, then one obtains a dynamics into

Set

by composition. In the classical analytic approach to dynamics, the functor
\tilde{\Phi}
is replaced by a continuous family of self-maps. As a result, it is easier to switch between the 4 interpretations. In our categorical setting, we need the crucial Assumptions on enrichment to make the connect between Interpretation 1 and 2. This identification of the structural components allows a synthetic approach to dynamical systems theory.

Assumptions
1
and
2
enable a description of the dynamics but that only captures the semigroup property. Depending on the context such as those listed in Table
1
, one often needs to establish the dependence of the dynamics law on time as continuous, linear, measurable or differentiable. Thus there is a need to extend the mathematical properties encoded within context
\Context
to the correspondence
t\mapsto\Phi(t)
as well. A general functor
\tilde{\Phi}:\mathcal{T}\to\Context
from Assumption
1
does not respect the
\Context
-enriched structure of
\mathcal{T}
. As a result,
\tilde{\Phi}
does not automatically lead to a morphism involving the time object
\mathbb{T}
within the category
\Context
. What we need is the concept of an
enriched functor
.

An
enriched functor

F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
between two categories enriched over a monoidal category
\mathcal{M}
is an ordinary functor such that between any two pairs of objects
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, there is an induced
\mathcal{M}
morphism

F_{x,x^{\prime}}:\mathcal{X}\left(x;x^{\prime}\right)\to\mathcal{Y}\left(Fx;Fx^{\prime}\right),

(15)

which respects unital composition :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}\left[Fx\;;\;Fx\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\left[x\;;\;x\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Fx}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{x,x}}

(16)

and compositionality :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\left[x^{\prime}\;;\;x^{\prime\prime}\right]\otimes\mathcal{X}\left[x\;;\;x^{\prime}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}\left[x\;;\;x^{\prime\prime}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}\left[Fx^{\prime}\;;\;Fx^{\prime\prime}\right]\otimes\mathcal{X}\left[Fx\;;\;Fx^{\prime}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}\left[Fx\;;\;Fx^{\prime\prime}\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}}\otimes F_{x,x^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{x,x^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{Fx,Fx^{\prime},Fx^{\prime\prime}}}

(17)

When
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{M}=\Context
and
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{T}
, then
\mathcal{X}
has only one object. Let
\Omega
be its image under
F
. Then (
15
) comprises of only one morphism

F^{\sharp}:\mathbb{T}\to\Endo(\Omega),

(18)

and the conditions (
16
) and (
17
) become :

(19)

Let
F,G:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be two enriched functors. Then an
enriched natural transformation

\omega:F\Rightarrow G
is a family of morphisms

\omega_{x}:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{Y}\left[Fx\;;\;Gx\right],\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{X},

(21)

We would like to assume that
\tilde{\Phi}
is an enriched functor. We would require both its domain
\mathcal{T}
and co-domain
\Context
to be categories enriched over the same monoidal category, which in this case is also
\Context
. The domain
\mathcal{T}
is enriched by Assumption
2
. The codomain
\Context
is automatically enriched, as observed below :

Lemma 4.1
.

A closed monoidal category is enriched over itself. The internal hom functor plays the role of the enrichment.

Lemma
4.1
is a standard result and may be found in
(
Kelly1982enriched
, Sec 1.6)
. This observation enables the additional assumption :

Assumption 4
.

The functor
\tilde{\Phi}
from Assumption
1
is an enriched functor.

Assumption
4
enables the semigroup action
\tilde{\Phi}
to be interpreted as a parameterized family of self-maps. Note that every category is enriched over

Set

, and so is every ordinary functor. Thus Assumptions
2
,
3
and
4
will be satisfied if
\Context=\textbf{ \text{Set} }
. Given an enriched functor
\tilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{T}\to\Context
, equations (
15
), (
16
) and (
17
) imply the existence of an object
\Omega
of
\Context
and a morphism
\Psi^{\sharp}:\mathbb{T}\to\Endo(\Omega)
such that the following commutations hold :

\hbox to111.44pt{\vbox to51.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 55.72162pt\lower-25.48613pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-55.72162pt}{-19.3264pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\Psi^{\sharp}
the
parametric form
of the enriched functor

.

(23)

Lemma
4.2
follows from the basic theory of enriched categories. One needs to place
\mathcal{T}
and
\Context
in the role of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively, in the definition of enriched functors. Take
\tilde{\Phi}
as
F
in (
2
),
x,x^{\prime}
to be the unique object of
\mathcal{T}
, and
y,y^{\prime}
to be the image
\Omega
of
\tilde{\Phi}
. The details of the proof can be found in Section
9.2
. Any morphism
\Psi^{\sharp}:\mathbb{T}\to\Endo{\Omega}
which also satisfies (
22
) will be called a
left action
.
Our first main result is that the shift morphism along the path space is the flow representation of an enriched functor :

Theorem 6

(Semigroup action of shift operator)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. The shift morphism from (
13
) describes a left action of
\mathcal{T}
on the object
\PathF(\Omega)
of
\Context
. Thus it corresponds uniquely to an enriched functor from
\mathcal{T}
to
\Context
, with domain
\PathF(\Omega)
.

Theorem
6
is proved in Section
9.4
. Although the shift flow is constructed for a prefixed object
\Omega
as in (
13
), Theorem
6
indicates that it preserves the structure present within
\Context
. The shift flow is also intrinsic to an object
\Omega
, and we shall explore the functorial nature of this relation later. Theorem
6
establishes the the shift flow
\shift
to be an object of the following category –

Definition 8

(Enriched semigroup actions)
.

The category of enriched semigroup actions
\mathbb{F}_{\text{enrich}}\left(\mathcal{T};\Context\right)
based on the context category
\Context
and time semigroup
\mathcal{T}
contains as objects enriched functors from
\mathcal{T}
to
\Context
, and enriched natural transformations as morphisms.

In our case
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{T}
which has only one object. So in diagram (
21
) we have

\begin{split}&x=x^{\prime}\\
&\mathcal{Y}\left(Gx;Gx^{\prime}\right)=\Endo(\Omega^{\prime}),\\
&\mathcal{Y}\left(Fx;Gx\right)=\mathcal{Y}\left(Fx;Gx^{\prime}\right)=\mathcal{Y}\left(Fx^{\prime};Gx^{\prime}\right)=\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}},\\
&\mathcal{Y}\left(Fx;Fx^{\prime}\right)=\Endo(\Omega).\end{split}

Consider two objects
\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{\Phi^{\prime}}
in
\mathbb{F}_{\text{enrich}}\left(\mathcal{T};\Context\right)
with domains
\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}
respectively. An
\mathbb{F}_{\text{enrich}}\left(\mathcal{T};\Context\right)
-morphism between them is a morphism
h:\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
such that its right-adjoint
h^{\sharp}:1_{\Context}\to\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}
satisfies :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Endo(\Omega^{\prime})\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}\otimes\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}\otimes\Endo(\Omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime\sharp}\otimes h^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{{\mathbb{T}}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\sharp}\otimes\tilde{\Phi}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

(24)

In
\mathbb{F}_{\text{enrich}}\left(\mathcal{T};\Context\right)
, the composition of two morphisms
h:\tilde{\Phi}\to\tilde{\Phi^{\prime}}
and
h^{\prime}:\tilde{\Phi^{\prime}}\to\tilde{\Phi^{\prime\prime}}
is the dashed arrow created by composition below :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}\otimes 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega^{\prime\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\Omega^{\prime}\,;\,\Omega^{\prime\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}\otimes\left[\Omega\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}\circ h)^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{1_{\Context}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h^{\prime\sharp}\otimes h^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

(25)

There are two other categories of dynamics we can build :

Definition 9

(Parameterized endomorphisms)
.

The category of parameterized endomorphisms
\left[\text{Endo}\left(\Context\right)\right]_{\text{para}}
based on the context category
\Context
and time semigroup
\mathcal{T}
contains as objects morphisms
\Psi^{\sharp}:\mathbb{T}\to\Endo\left(\Omega\right)
which satisfy (
22
).

The interpretation in Definition
9
forfeits the algebraic property within the dynamics law and directly encodes the time dependence as a morphism within
\Context
. Thus the type of dependence on time, namely linear, continuous, measurable or differentiable, can be inferred directly from the context
\Context
. The morphisms and compositions within this category will be described in the upcoming Theorem
7
. They will be defined indirectly via the following well defined category :

Definition 10

(Category of left-actions)
.

The category
\left[\text{Left-action}\left(\Context\right)\right]
of flows or left-actions based on the context category
\Context
and time semigroup
\mathcal{T}
contains as objects morphisms
\Psi:\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega\to\Omega
which satisfy (
23
). A morphism between two objects
\left(\Omega,\Psi\right)
and
\left(\Omega^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime}\right)
corresponds to a morphism
h:\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
such that the following commutation holds :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\otimes\Omega^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\prime}}

(26)

Similar to the interpretation in Definition
9
, Definition
10
also interprets the dynamics law as a morphism from time-space object
\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega
into
\Omega
. Unlike the former, the latter description explicitly encodes the dependence of the dynamics on both the state as well as time objects.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold. Then :

1.

The collection of objects and morphisms in
\mathbb{F}_{\text{enrich}}\left(\mathcal{T};\Context\right)
from Definition
8
, and in
\left[\text{Left-action}\left(\Context\right)\right]
from Definition
10
are categories.

2.

These two categories are isomorphic.

3.

There is a bijective correspondence between the objects of these categories and that of the collection
\left[\text{Endo}\left(\Context\right)\right]_{\text{para}}
from Definition
9
.

4.

These three collections form three isomorphic categories as shown in (
1
), offering differing descriptions of dynamics in a context
\Context
.

5.

The correspondence between
\tilde{\Phi}
and
\Phi^{\sharp}
from (
11
); and between
\Phi^{\sharp}
and
\Phi
from (
12
) are functorial.

Theorem
7
is proved in Section
9.5
. Each description of a dynamical system in Diagram (
1
) differs in the nature of
\tilde{\Phi}
. Theorem
7
states that not only is there a one-to-one correspondence between these different
\tilde{\Phi}
s, but also between the morphisms which bind them within their own categories. Theorem
7
thus enables us to freely chose a particular categorical presentation of a dynamical system that suits our purpose. We next refine our understanding of the shift functor.

Theorem 8
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
6
hold. Then the correspondence of
\Omega
with
\shift_{\Omega}
is the result of a functor

\shift:\Context\to\left[\text{Left-action}\left(\Context\right)\right].

The action of this functor on morphisms is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega^{\prime}\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,f\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift_{\Omega^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,f\right]_{\text{int}}}

(27)

Equation (
27
) is proved in Section
9.9
. Our next important result is :

Theorem 9

(Semigroup action of Transfer operator)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
holds. The morphism
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}
from (
14
) satisfies (
22
). Thus
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}
is the flow-form of an enriched functor
\mathcal{T}\to\Context
whose domain is the path space
\PathF(\Omega)
corresponding to the domain
\Omega
of the original functor
\tilde{\Phi}
from Assumption
1
.

Theorem
9
is proved in Section
9.6
. Theorem
9
shows that the transfer operator is an object of the category
\left[\text{Endo}\left(\Context\right)\right]_{\text{para}}
. By Theorem
7
, the transfer operator can also be interpreted as a unique enriched functor from
\mathcal{T}
to
\Context
, as well as a unique left
\mathbb{T}
-action on
\Context
.

We have thus seen three different kinds of dynamical systems within a context category
\Context
satisfying Assumptions
2
,
3
and
4
. The first is a general enriched functor from
\mathcal{T}
. The second is the shift-flow on path spaces, and the third is a the transfer operator. The latter two are both secondary dynamical systems on path spaces. We shall see in the next section how these to leads to deeper insights into the original dynamics
\tilde{\Phi}
. Our categorification of the collection of enriched functors, parametric dynamics and flows will turn out to be useful.

5  Path-spaces as dynamics

We have so far seen multiple descriptions of a dynamical system - as a group action, a flow, and a parameterized endomorphism. Figure
4
summarizes how our ground assumptions make these descriptions interchangeable. Each of these descriptions come handy under different circumstances. In this section we shall look at a prime example. Recall (
14
) in which the transfer operator morphism
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}
is derived as an adjoint. Also recall the
\shift
morphism from (
13
). One can obtain other adjoints of these morphisms :

Although these two morphisms
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}^{\flat}
and
\shift^{\flat}
are between the same pair of objects, they describe two completely different types of dynamics. These morphisms come together in the following more general result.

Equalizing dynamics

Let
X
be an object in
\Context
and
F,G
be two pre-flows on
X
as shown below on the left :

(28)

The morphisms have their respective right adjoints, as shown above on the right. Construct their equalizer as follows :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\flat}}

(29)

We now have :

Theorem 10
.

Suppose Assumptions
2
,
3
and
4
hold. Assume the morphisms and objects in (
28
) and (
29
). Then there exists a unique morphism
F\cap G:\mathbb{T}\otimes E\to E

such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\cap G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

(30)

Moreover, if
F,G
are left-actions of
\mathcal{T}
, then so is
F\cap G
.

Theorem
10
is proved in Section
9.7
.
The morphism
F\cap G
so constructed is an equalizer of the dynamics under the pre-flows
F,G
respectively. Any pre-flow has an alternate and equivalent description as a morphism from the state-space
X
into the path-space
\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,X\right]_{\text{int}}
. The pre-flow
F\cap G
has as its domain that subspace of
X
which generates the same paths under both
F
and
G
. The pre-flow
F\cap G
thus encodes the commonality between
F
and
G
, in terms of the paths they produce from each state.

The most interesting application of Theorem
10
is to take :

X=\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}},\quad F=\shift_{\Omega},\quad G=\text{Tr}_{\Phi}.

The action of
\tilde{\Phi}
embedded as a flow in the path space
\PathF(\Omega)

Figure 5
:
The subshift functor. As shown in Figure
4
, the most basic description of a dynamical system is as the action of a semigroup
\mathcal{T}
on a domain
\Omega
. In the usual contexts of set-theoretic or topological dynamics, it is well known that each state
\omega\in\Omega
leads to a trajectory under the dynamics. In a general category-
\Context
these trajectories or paths together form a new space
\PathF(\Omega)
called the
path-space
, which is again a member of category-
\Context
. Each trajectory is equipped with a natural motion along it. These motions collectively create a dynamics on
\PathF(\Omega)
. Thus overall one derives a new dynamical system from the existing dynamics. We describe this construction for a general category
\Context
, which generalizes the set-theoretic and topological cases. We also show that this construction is functorial.

Theorem 11

(Subshift representation of a dynamical system)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold, and
\Context
has equalizers. Assume the notation of (
31
). Then

1.

there is a equalizer

q:E_{\Phi}\to\PathF(\Omega)

of the morphisms
\shift_{\Omega}^{\sharp}
,
\text{Tr}_{\Phi}^{\sharp}
from
\PathF(\Omega)
to
\PathF\left(\PathF(\Omega)\right)
.

2.

E_{\Phi}
is a subobject of
\PathF(\Omega)
.

3.

There exists a unique morphism

\shift_{\Phi}:\mathbb{T}\otimes E_{\Phi}\to E_{\Phi},

(32)

such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes E_{\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift_{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift_{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\text{Tr}_{\Phi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift}

(33)

4.

The morphism
\shift_{\Phi}
in (
32
) is a left action of
\mathbb{T}
.

(34)

where
q
and
q^{\prime}
are universal morphisms of the equalizer.

Theorem
11
is proved in Section
9.8
. Every trajectory of
\tilde{\Phi}
is a point in the space
\PathF(\Omega)
. However not every point in
\PathF(\Omega)
is a trajectory of
\tilde{\Phi}
. Theorem
11
explicitly reconstructs the dynamics of
\tilde{\Phi}
’s orbit space as an embedded dynamics within
\PathF(\Omega)
. This essence of Theorem
12
was distilled earlier in Corollary
4
. The flow
\shift_{\Phi}
from (
32
) thus leads to a semigroup action

\shift_{\Phi}:\mathcal{T}\to\Endo\left(E_{\Phi}\right).

(35)

The surprising revelation of Theorem
11
that goes beyond Theorem
10
is that the correspondence of a flow with its subshift is also functorial. In other words the operation
E
is an endofunctor on the category of left actions. This functor will be revealed to be even more informative. We next show that the semigroup action in (
34
) is an isomorphic copy of the semigroup action of
\tilde{\Phi}
.

Theorem 12
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold, and additionally, and
\mathbb{T}
be a commutative monoid. Let
\Phi^{\flat}:\Omega\to\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}
be the adjoint of a
\mathbb{T}
-action
\Phi:\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega\to\Omega
. Then
\Phi^{\flat}
coincides with the equalizer
q
from (
31
). Consequently the subshift domain
E_{\Phi}
is isomorphic to
\Omega
.

Theorem
12
is proved in Section
9.10
. One of the major revelations of Theorem
12
was announced earlier in Corollary
5
. A crucial property of the time-0 evaluation morphism (
45
) is as a left inverse to the flow :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{eval}_{0}}

(36)

This commutation is a direct consequence of the unital identity (
23
) of the flow
\Phi
. Theorem
11
, Theorem
12
and (
36
) combine to give

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes E_{\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{T}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift_{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\Phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shift_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{eval}_{0}}

This indicates that irrespective of
\tilde{\Phi}
and its nature as a flow or semi-flow, the subshift representation retains an isomorphic copy of
\Omega
at each time instant.

This completes our investigation of the theoretical outcomes of the four ground assumptions - Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
. These structural assumptions enable not only the primary description of a dynamical system as a semigroup action, but also provides additional structure
\mathbb{T}
to the time semigroup
\mathcal{T}
, provides three other equivalent descriptions of mathematics each capturing a different role, enables the notions of paths, path-space and subshifts. We next examine how it also allows the notion of states or points and state-preservation to be incorporated within the categorical framework.

6  States of a dynamics functor

Dynamical systems theory is primarily interested in the asymptotic properties of trajectories, and on invariant points, subsets and measures. All these notions are built upon the primitive notions of states and invariance. In this section we examine how the notion of states arise naturally in the enriched category framework. An important assumption that enables a complete discussion of states is

Assumption 5
.

The unit element
1_{\Context}
is terminal.

Recall that being
terminal
means that for every object
x\in\Context
, there is a unique morphism from
x
to
1_{\Context}
. While Assumption
5
is required for general monoidal categories, it automatically holds true for Cartesian categories, as implied by the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Cartesian closed category. Then an object is the unit object iff it is the terminal object.

With this Assumption in mind, we define :

Definition 11

(States)
.

A
state
of an object
\Omega
in a monoidal category
\Context
is any morphism
\omega:1_{\Context}\to\Omega
.

This concept of state is similar to the concept of an
element

(
Flori_Topos_2013
;
Barr1986
, e.g.)
in a general category, in which a monoidal product is replaced by a categorical product, and the monoidal unital element
1_{\Context}
by a terminal element. In our context
\Context
, we thus interpret a state to be any morphism from the distinguished element
1_{\Context}
. The unit cum terminal object makes possible an elementary functor, which is of fundamental importance :

Definition 12

(Terminal functor)
.

If Assumption
5
holds then there is an enriched functor
\Delta:\mathcal{T}\to\Context
with constant value
1_{\Context}
. The connecting morphism as defined in (
18
) is the unique terminal morphism shown below on the left :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\sharp}}

and the commutation relation (
19
) trivially holds, as each of the objects in the second row become
1_{\Context}
.

The connecting morphism
\Delta^{\sharp}
satisfies the following commutation

(37)

This leads to one of our new constructs :

Definition 13

(Enriched stationary state)
.

An enriched stationary state of a functor
\tilde{\Phi}
as in Assumption
4
is an enriched natural transformation
\tilde{\omega}:\Delta\Rightarrow\tilde{\Phi}
.

An enriched natural transformation
\tilde{\omega}:\Delta\Rightarrow\tilde{\Phi}
as above has only one connecting morphism which is

\omega_{*}:1_{\Context}\to\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}},

(38)

Thus the enriched natural transformation
\tilde{\omega}
leads to an element
\omega_{*}
of the internal-element object
\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}
. Since
\Context
is closed,
\omega_{*}
has a left-dual, which is

^{*}\left(\omega_{*}\right):1_{\Context}\otimes 1_{\Context}\to\Omega.

(39)

This morphism can be trivially combined with the unitor isomorphism for
1_{\Context}
to get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}\otimes 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}\otimes\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{1}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{*}\left(\omega_{*}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}\otimes\omega_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{eval}_{1,\Omega}}

(40)

The right commuting triangle follows from Lemma
9.1
which will be presented later. It expresses a left dual in terms of the evaluation morphism. Thus an enriched stationary state leads to an ordinary state
\omega
of
\Omega
.

Theorem 13

(Characterization of stationary states)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
hold. Then the following are equivalent :

1.

\tilde{\Phi}
has an enriched stationary state
\tilde{\omega}
.

2.

There is a morphism
\omega_{*}
as in (
38
) which satisfies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Endo(\Omega)\otimes\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}\otimes 1_{\Context}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\sharp}\otimes\omega_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\omega}_{*}\otimes\Delta^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(41)

3.

There is a morphism
\omega_{*}
as in (
38
) which satisfies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Endo(\Omega)\otimes\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[1_{\Context}\,;\,\Omega\right]_{\text{int}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\sharp}\otimes\omega_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{*}}

(42)

Any of these equivalent conditions imply

4.

There is a morphism
\omega_{*}
as in (
38
) whose dual
\omega
as in (
40
) satisfies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Context}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\otimes\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

(43)

Theorem
13
is proved in Section
9.11
. Equation (
43
) is the classical representation of a stationary state of a pre-flow
\Phi
. One of the consequence of Theorem
13
is that an enriched stationary state is also a stationary state in the ordinary sense. Such an implication is desirable since our objective is to establish enriched functors as an extension of the notion of ordinary dynamical systems. Being an extension should mean that the associated notions such as states as semi-group actions are also extended.

The notion of state leads to the notion of orbits. We utilize Theorem
7
which makes the various descriptions of dynamics interchangeable. We shall be using the flow-form
\Phi:\mathbb{T}\otimes\Omega\to\Omega
of
\tilde{\Phi}
. Consider any state
\omega
of
\Omega
as shown below on the left :
